But more still: how will you infer straightway from the long duration of an institution its goodness, and that it deserves to be retained further, when there are also institutions which for centuries were beneficial, indeed even necessary, and only now, owing to altered circumstances, have become dispensable, nay, a hindrance to the further progress of mankind? Can I cite a more remarkable and decisive example of this than the Mosaic religion? And so, if you wish to act rationally, O men, you may not, from the duration of an institution, nor from its wide diffusion, nor even from the number of defenders it still finds at this hour (how great was not the number of defenders of which heathendom could once boast over against Christianity?) — decide as to its worth and as to the important question whether you are to vote for its further retention or rather to summon up all your forces for its final abolition? On grounds of an altogether different kind, from the consideration of all the nearer as well as the remoter effects that an institution brings in its train, from regard to all the changes that would result from its abolition, from its inner nature as well as from all the external circumstances and relations that have an influence here, must you decide what is to be done in such a case. But once you have clearly recognised that, and in what way, an establishment is detrimental to the virtue and happiness of the human race, and [p. 16] which other is to be put in its place: then regard it as a duty not to be evaded by anything to work towards the displacement of the harmful by the decidedly better. And believe with confidence that this duty is not altered in the least, even when you see that many an attempt to introduce this improvement has already remained without success. Or what would you conclude from such experiences? That it did not happen without God’s will that those attempts at an overthrow were frustrated? That I grant you in advance, but only in just the sense in which you can say even of the most atrocious crime that it did not pass from the realm of possibility into that of actuality without God’s will, namely not without his permissive will. Now just as little as a crime ceases to be what it is merely because it could not come about without God’s permission: so little do perverse institutions cease to be what they are when God — who alone surveys the connection of the whole, lets centuries pass without bringing about the means that would suffice for their abolition. Nay, let even more happen, let the rarest events combine to help up again an institution that was already close to its overthrow: even this changes nothing in your duty to set yourselves against what you have clearly recognised as an institution detrimental to the well-being and morality of men. What, or would it perhaps accord with the laws of your reason, this guide given you by God himself, to recognise in the concurrence of such events the signs that he has taken the establishment in question under his protection, that he wishes to see it upheld, and upheld moreover through your co-operation? So you might well conclude if it were a question of an establishment which, by all that you are able to discern, presents itself as beneficial for mankind. But if the very opposite is the case, then you are [p. 17] never entitled to such a conclusion, even if you are utterly unable to explain for what other purpose those events are meant. For does not every single thing in the world have immeasurably many effects as well as causes? Cannot, then, the events that so astonish you also be grounded in a very necessary way in the connection of the whole, and likewise have their beneficial, though to you imperceptible, effects for the future? Nothing, therefore, compels us, indeed nothing even permits us, to say that they are there to be a sign to us that God wills us to do what, according to all our insight, appears to us as something harmful and manifestly evil. — You reply, to be sure, that we might after all be mistaken in judging whether an establishment really has such ruinous consequences. And this possibility I will again grant you; nevertheless it remains certain that we are free of all responsibility when we follow what, according to our best insight, presents itself to us as good and of common benefit; and equally certain that God, if he wishes to make his counsel known to us by signs, will not and cannot give ambiguous signs, signs resting on a mere perhaps. If, then, we see that an institution which after the most mature deliberation we can only declare ruinous is preserved by the concurrence of the most unusual occurrences, just when we hoped to have displaced it by something better: let us confess that we cannot, it is true, state with certainty why this came about, but that we do know with sufficient certainty that it did not happen in order to deter us from further attempts, but that it happened so that in future we should apply ever more deliberation and caution. We do not, therefore, fear that we have done what is displeasing to God when we have acted according to our best knowledge with the purest intention; we do not believe that the apparently favourable failure of our undertaking proves [p. 18] by the deed itself that we acted against God’s will, least of all against a will of God recognisable by us beforehand and declared to us; but after we have examined everything anew and found no reason to alter our views, let us proceed untiringly to new attempts; let us use the experiences we have gained only for what they can be used for, namely to fight in future still more cautiously and, if possible, with stronger weapons. But yet another question arises, which we must discuss in a few words. Even if the proposition that everything actual is rational, and conversely, alters nothing in the difference that exists between the morally good and evil: will not at least the difference that, according to the common view, we make between events which we call fortunate or gladdening and others which we call unfortunate or saddening undergo a considerable change from the higher point of view that faith in God opens to us? And this question I must answer in the affirmative, but in such a way that I maintain: faith in God takes from the events we call fortunate not the least of their gladdening quality for us, but rather heightens it; while the saddening character that the unfortunate events have is indeed diminished by this faith, and diminished to a degree the more considerable, the more vividness and strength we are able to give to it. For that we rejoice in an event is certainly in no way forbidden or spoiled for us by our faith in God or by the proposition that everything actual is rational, and conversely; unless perhaps what gladdens us in an event were in contradiction with the laws of morality; in which case, however, every rightminded person would not wish to rejoice and would not rejoice, even if no faith in God forbade it. Apart from this case, we can rejoice all the more heartily in every event that we have hitherto been accustomed to call a fortunate one, I mean in every event that promises us agreeable things not [p. 19] only in its immediate but also in all the more remote consequences that our eye is able to reach, the less we have to fear that mere chance will destroy our happiness again, or even that a maliciously gloating being who directs our destinies gladdens us for a time only in order suddenly to wound us all the more deeply through disappointed hopes. So much concerning the events that bring happiness; as regards those, however, which we are accustomed to call unfortunate, that is, those which at least in their nearest effects are painful to our sensibility: it is surely natural that at their onset we grieve, and are unable altogether to suppress the wish that we might be spared them, or at least had already got through them; this is natural, I say, even if we possess faith in God and therefore do not in the least doubt that everything whatever that is and happens is rational, that even from the sufferings and pains we now experience predominantly beneficial consequences for ourselves and others will spring, and that not a single one of these bitter sensations could be spared us with greater advantage. This, I repeat, is natural, because painful sensations cannot be abolished and annihilated by the mere idea of a happiness that will only sprout from them in the future. Or how could we perhaps ward off the sadness that takes possession of our heart when we must look on as a devastating plague spreads through whole continents, and not only threatens our own life but also carries off thousands of our brothers by a dreadful death? how could we, how should we, not mourn, even if we have no reason to reproach ourselves that in the end we ourselves too had in some way a nearer or more remote share in its spread? But if faith in God cannot transform an event sad in itself into a gladdening one, or even [p. 20] into an altogether indifferent one: yet it is evident, and was already remarked above, that it can make the bearing of it uncommonly easier for us. The more familiar we make to ourselves the consoling thought, what God does is well done, the more strength and vividness we procure for it by making use of all the cases in which its truth can become intuitively clear to us; the more unshakeable the firmness we give to faith in God by resolving into their nothingness all doubts and objections opposed to it: the more calmly and composedly shall we go to meet every fate against which our sensuous nature rebels, and know how to bear it.

p. 21

Verhält es ſich wirklich nur ſo mit Sinn und Auslegung des Satzes, daß alles Wirkliche vernünftig und umgekehrt ſei: ſo mag nun Jeder ſelbſt urtheilen, ob es ein Lobſpruch einer Philoſophie ſei, wenn von derſelben geſagt wird, „ſie habe Friede mit der Wirklichkeit geſchloſſen; ſie wolle nichts ſchaffen, nichts umſtalten; ſie wolle nicht klüger ſein als der Weltgeiſt, d. h. die Vernunft der Menſchheit es eben jetzt iſt"? — Nein, wahrlich! ein Lobſpruch wäre ſo etwas nur, wenn der Satz, daß alles Wirkliche vernünftig iſt, in einem Sinne gälte, in dem er die handgreiflichſte Unwahrheit iſt, daß nämlich alle Einrichtungen, die unter uns Menſchen zur Wirklichkeit gelangen, und daß eben ſo alle durch ſolche Einrichtungen ſich kundgebende Begriffe und Meinungen mindeſtens dann, wenn ſie ſich einer großen Allgemeinheit erfreuen, zweckmäßig und vernünftig ſein müßten. Wohl behaupten wir ſelbſt und werden nie aufhören zu behaupten, daß es allerdings einen Fall gebe, in welchem wir mit vieler Zuverſicht ſchließen dürfen, daß eine Lehre vernünftig und wahr ſei, blos weil wir ſie all- *) Wie z. B. Dr. Mayer von der Hegel'ſchen Philoſophie erſt kürzlich in einer ſehr populär gehaltenen Schrift, in einem Schreiben an eine Dame über Philoſophie (Berlin 1837) geſagt hat. [p. 21] gemein ausgebreitet finden. Es iſt der Fall, wenn es ſich um eine Frage handelt, welche durch bloſe Vernunft verbunden allenfalls mit Erfahrungen, die einem Jeden zu Gebote ſtehen, ſich zur Entſcheidung bringen läßt, und wenn dieſe Entſcheidung ſo lautet, daß ſie der Sinnlichkeit nicht ſchmeichelt, ſondern ihr vielmehr Abbruch thut. In einem ſolchen Falle iſt die übereinſtimmende Geſinnung der Menſchen aller Zeiten und Lande in der That eine Autorität, über die es keine größere gibt. Wo aber dieſe Bedingungen nicht ſtatt haben, wo wir von einer Lehre oder Einrichtung ſprechen, bei der entweder eine wirkliche Uebereinſtimmung Aller gar nicht vorhanden iſt, weil Tauſende unter den Weiſeren ihre Mißbilligung laut genug zu erkennen gaben, oder wenn zu der richtigen Beurtheilung der Sache gewiſſe eigenthümliche, den wenigſten Menſchen zu Gebote ſtehende Beobachtungen erforderlich wären, oder wenn ſich der Anhang, den eine Lehre und den gewiſſe ſich auf ſie ſtützende Einrichtungen finden, ſehr leicht erklären läßt aus den vielen Unbequemlichkeiten, welche mit ihrer Abänderung anfangs verbunden waren, oder wohl auch aus der Gefahr, die jeden anders Denkenden bedroht, wenn er ſeine Meinung laut werden läßt: dann iſt es in der That für Niemand ſchimpflicher und verantwortlicher als für den Philoſophen, nicht klüger ſein zu wollen, als es der Weltgeiſt, d. h. die große, die herrſchende Menge eben jetzt iſt. Wie ſoll je die Menſchheit klüger werden, als ſie es eben jetzt iſt, wenn ſelbſt der Philoſoph es ſich nicht herausnehmen will, in irgend einem Puncte anders zu denken als die große Menge eben jetzt denket? Wer ſoll's denn wagen, Verbeſſerungen in Vorſchlag zu bringen, wenn nicht einmal er, der Philoſoph etwas „erſchaffen", etwas zur Wirklichkeit Gelangtes „umſtalten“ will? wenn ſelbſt er einen Ruhm darin ſucht, mit der Wirklichkeit d. h. mit allem einmal Beſtehenden „Friede geſchlosſen zu haben“? Es war wohl freilich gefehlt, wenn Philoſophen oder beſſer geſagt, nur Menſchen, die dieſen ehrwürdigen Namen höchſt widerrechtlicher Weiſe ſich anmaßten, jede beſtehende Lehre und Ordnung blos darum, weil es die beſtehende war, [p. 22] umſtürzen wollten, ohne die ſorgfältigſte Ueberlegung vorausgeſchickt und ohne die vollgiltigſten Gründe dazu gefunden zu haben. Muß man denn aber aus einem Aeußerſten immer ins Andere fallen? Dürfen wir darum, weil ehedem Manche in ihrer Verbeſſerungsſucht allzuweit gingen, nun im geraden Gegentheil Frieden mit allem Beſtehenden ſchließen? — Daß eine gewiſſe Philoſophie ſehr viel Geſchmeidigkeit habe, und Alles, was ihr wollt, als wahr beweiſen könne, wußten wir lange ſchon; es war nicht nöthig, uns ihre Maxime, nur das als wahr aufzuſtellen, wovon ſie hoffen kann, daß es nicht ungern werde gehört und angenommen werden, ſo offen heraus zu ſagen. [p. 23] deque errore tot philosophorum, qui de bonis contrariisque rebus tantopere discrepant, ut cum plus uno verum esse non possit, jacere necesse ſit tot tam nobiles disciplinas- Cicero Acad, quaest. lib. IV.

If this really is the only way it stands with the sense and interpretation of the proposition that everything actual is rational, and conversely: then let each judge for himself whether it is praise of a philosophy when it is said of it that “3 it has made peace with actuality; it wishes to create nothing, to reshape nothing; it wishes to be no wiser than the world spirit, that is, the reason of mankind, is just now”? — No, truly! such a thing would be praise only if the proposition that everything actual is rational held in a sense in which it is the most palpable untruth, namely that all institutions that attain actuality among us human beings, and likewise all concepts and opinions making themselves known through such institutions, must be fit for their purpose and rational, at least when they enjoy great generality. We ourselves do indeed maintain, and shall never cease to maintain, that there is certainly one case in which we may conclude with much confidence that a doctrine is rational [p. 21] and true merely because we find it generally diffused. It is the case when the question at issue is one that can be brought to a decision by mere reason, combined if need be with experiences that are at everyone’s disposal, and when this decision runs in such a way that it does not flatter sensuality but rather impairs it. In such a case the concordant conviction of men of all times and lands is indeed an authority than which there is none greater. But where these conditions do not obtain, where we are speaking of a doctrine or institution in regard to which either an actual agreement of all is not present at all, because thousands among the wiser have made their disapproval known loudly enough, or where the correct judgement of the matter would require certain peculiar observations available to very few people, or where the following that a doctrine, and certain institutions resting upon it, find can very easily be explained by the many inconveniences that were at first bound up with changing it, or perhaps also by the danger that threatens everyone who thinks otherwise if he lets his opinion be heard: then indeed it is for no one more disgraceful and more culpable than for the philosopher not to wish to be wiser than the world spirit, that is, the great, the ruling multitude, is just now. How is mankind ever to become wiser than it is just now if even the philosopher will not presume to think otherwise on any point than the great multitude thinks just now? Who, then, is to dare to propose improvements, if not even he, the philosopher, will “create” anything, will “reshape” anything that has attained actuality? if even he seeks his glory in “having made peace” with actuality, that is, with everything once established? It was indeed a fault, to be sure, when philosophers, or better said, merely men who most unlawfully arrogated to themselves this venerable name, wished to overthrow every established doctrine [p. 22] and order merely because it was the established one, without first undertaking the most careful deliberation and without having found the most valid reasons for doing so. But must one always fall from one extreme into the other? Because some formerly went too far in their mania for improvement, may we now, in direct contrast, make peace with everything established? — That a certain philosophy has a great deal of suppleness, and can prove as true anything you like, we have long known; it was not necessary to tell us so openly its maxim of setting up as true only what it can hope will be heard and accepted not unwillingly. II. Ueber Hegel’s und seiner Anhänger Begriff von der Geschichte überhaupt und insbesondere von der Geschichte der Philosophie. — II. On Hegel’s and His Followers’ Concept of History in General and of the History of Philosophy in Particular [p. 23] Posthac potius de dissensionibus tantis summorum virorum disseramus, de obscuritate naturae deque errore tot philosophorum, qui de bonis contrariisque rebus tantopere discrepant, ut cum plus uno verum esse non possit, jacere necesse sit tot tam nobiles disciplinas. [Hereafter let us rather discuss the great disagreements among the most eminent men, the obscurity of nature, and the error of so many philosophers, who differ so widely about good things and their contraries that, since no more than one of them can be true, so many noble schools must necessarily fall to the ground.] Cicero, Academica, book IV.

p. 24

Unter den Lehren, durch welche Hegel ſeinem philoſophiſchen Syſteme Aufmerkſamkeit und Beifall ſelbſt bei einem Theile desjenigen gelehrten Publicums verſchaffte, das ſich ſonſt mit ſeiner höchſt ſonderbaren Methode des Philoſophirens nicht befreundete, ſtehet oben an die eigenthümliche Anſicht von der Geſchichte überhaupt und von der Geſchichte der Philoſophie insbesondere. Hegel hat, wie bekannt, die Behauptung aufgeſtellt, daß eine (philoſophiſche) Geſchichte der Menſchheit nichts Anderes zu erzählen habe, oder vielmehr (nach einer ſonderbaren Verwechslung der Geſchichte als der Erzählerin mit den Begebenheiten, welche ſie zu erzählen hat) daß die Geſchichte der Menſchheit ſchon an ſich ſelbſt nichts Anderes ſei als die in der Zeit ſich vollendende Entwickelung des Weltgeiſtes, wodurch derſelbe allmälig zu ſeinem eigenen Selbſtbewußtſein oder zur Freiheit gelange. Jeder folgende Zuſtand der Menſchheit ſei im Grunde nie etwas Anderes als ein aus allen früheren hervorgegangener Fortſchritt in dem vernünftigen und nothwendigen Gange des Weltgeiſtes. Eben ſo ſoll auch die Geſchichte der Philoſophie nichts Anderes ſein, als ihre eigene Entwickelung. Hierbei ſei es als etwas Unbezweifelbares, ja Nothwendiges vorauſzuſetzen, daß jedes der Zeit nach ſpäter auftretende Syſtem nichts Anderes als [p. 24] eine zeitgemäße Vervollkommnung des ihm unmittelbar Vorhergegangenen iſt. Mehr noch, ein jedes, mindeſtens jedes philoſophiſche Syſtem, welches zu ſeiner Zeit eine gewiſſe Geltung erlangte, iſt eigentlich ſchon als vollkommen anzuſehen, vollkommen nämlich für das Zeitalter, in welchem es eben auftrat und galt; wir haben es nicht blos als eine Vorarbeit zur Löſung des ganzen, der Philoſophie obliegenden Problem's, ſondern ſchon als die völlige Löſung desſelben, doch nur für dieſe Zeit zu betrachten. Näher noch wird verlangt, daß ſich die ganze Geſchichte der Philoſophie in lauter Gruppen zu je drei Syſtemen zerlegen laſſe, in deren Einem (dem früheren) Etwas geſetzt, im zweiten Etwas demſelben entgegengeſetzt und in dem dritten Beides in eine höhere Einheit vereiniget wird. Einem Schüler Hegels, von Cieskowski*) iſt dies Alles noch nicht wiſſenſchaftlich genug. Nach ihm ſoll die trichotomiſche Gliederung auch in der Geſchichte (nicht nur der Philoſophie, ſondern der ganzen Menſchheit) eine ſo durchgängige Anwendung finden, daß keine andere neben ihr Platz greifen darf. Darum muß denn auch die Geſchichte, wenn man ſie erſt in ihrer Totalität nimmt, d. h. nebſt der Vergangenheit und Gegenwart auch die Zukunft dazu nimmt, in drei Perioden zerfallen, deren erſte die thetiſche, die zweite die antithetiſche, die letzte die ſynthetiſche iſt, Die erſte war die des Alterthums, die zweite die chriſtlich - germaniſche Welt und an dem Eingange der dritten ſollen wir eben ſtehen. In der erſten ſoll das Schöne, in der zweiten die Wahrheit das Herrſchende geweſen ſein, in der dritten ſoll die That zur Herrſchaft gelangen. Nebſt dieſem Organismus des Nacheinanderſeins ſoll es auch einen des Nebeneinanderſeins und endlich des Concentrirtſeins geben. Vermöge des zweiten ſpiegeln alle zu einer Zeit coexiſtirenden Völker Vergangenheit ſowohl als Zukunft ab; vermöge des letzteren ſind die großen Männer die Repräſentanten der allgemeinen hiſtoriſchen Idee ſowohl *) Prolegomena zur Hiſtorioſophie. Berlin 1838. [p. 25] als des beſtimmten Charakters ihres eigenen Volkes; daher man (nach C.) insgemein die Geſchichte eines ganzen Volks erzählt hat, wenn man nur die ſeiner großen Männer, der Oberhäupter desſelben z. B. erzählt hat, ꝛc. Hinſichtlich der Philoſophie meint C., dieſe habe ihren höchſten Punct in der Gegenwart ſo ſehr erreicht, daß ſie von nun an es ſich müſſe gefallen laſſen, über ſich ſelbſt hinauszugehen, und aus der Höhe der Theorie in die Gefilde der Praxis hinabzuſteigen oder angewandt zu werden, Ihr nächſtes Schickſal werde ſein, ſich zu populariſiren, „ſich in die Tiefe zu verflachen.“

Among the doctrines by which Hegel won attention and applause for his philosophical system even from a part of that learned public which otherwise did not take to his highly peculiar method of philosophising, the foremost place belongs to his distinctive view of history in general and of the history of philosophy in particular. Hegel, as is well known, put forward the assertion that a (philosophical) history of mankind has nothing else to narrate, or rather (by a peculiar confusion of history as the narrator with the events which it has to narrate) that the history of mankind is already in itself nothing other than the development of the world spirit completing itself in time, whereby that spirit gradually attains to its own self-consciousness, or to freedom. Every succeeding state of mankind is, he held, at bottom never anything other than a progress, issuing from all the earlier ones, in the rational and necessary course of the world spirit. In the same way the history of philosophy too is supposed to be nothing other than its own development. Here it is to be presupposed as something indubitable, indeed necessary, that every system appearing later in time is [p. 24] nothing other than a timely perfecting of the one immediately preceding it. More than that: every system, at least every philosophical system that attained a certain currency in its time, is really to be regarded as already perfect, perfect, that is, for the age in which it appeared and held sway; we are to regard it not merely as a preparatory labour towards the solution of the whole problem incumbent on philosophy, but as already the complete solution of that problem, though only for that time. More specifically, it is demanded that the whole history of philosophy be divisible into nothing but groups of three systems each, in the first of which (the earlier) something is posited, in the second something is opposed to it, and in the third both are united in a higher unity. For one of Hegel’s pupils, von Cieskowski, all this is not yet scientific4 enough. According to him the trichotomous articulation is to find so thoroughgoing an application in history as well (not only of philosophy, but of all mankind) that no other may gain ground beside it. Therefore history too, when one takes it in its totality, that is, when one includes the future along with the past and the present, must fall into three periods, the first of which is the thetic, the second the antithetic, the last the synthetic. The first was that of antiquity, the second the Christian-Germanic world, and we are supposed to stand just now at the entrance of the third. In the first the Beautiful is supposed to have been dominant, in the second Truth; in the third the Deed is to attain dominion. Besides this organism of succession there is also supposed to be one of juxtaposition and finally one of concentration. By virtue of the second, all the peoples coexisting at one time mirror past as well as future; by virtue of the last, great men are the representatives of the [p. 25] universal historical idea as well as of the particular character of their own people; hence (according to C.) one has as a rule narrated the history of a whole people if one has only narrated that of its great men, of its rulers for example, etc. As regards philosophy, C. holds that it has reached its highest point in the present to such a degree that from now on it must consent to go beyond itself and to descend from the heights of theory into the fields of practice, or to be applied. Its next destiny, he says, will be to popularise itself, “to become shallow in depth.”

p. 26

Es iſt nicht zu verkennen, daß in allen dieſen Behauptungen etwas Beſtechendes liege. ) Denn um nur dabei, was von der Philoſophie geſagt wird zu verweilen: wie angenehm iſt es zu denken, daß wir ſo leichten Kaufes, nämlich nur wenn wir uns mit dem Syſteme, das in der Gegenwart eben das Herrſchende iſt, bekannt machen, zu dem Beſitze der für dieſe Zeit vollkommenſten, ja (nach C.) ſogar der abſolut vollkommenen Philoſophie gelangen? Auch macht man es uns wahrlich nicht ſchwer, unter den mehreren Syſtemen, welche in unſern Tagen hie und da aufgeſtellt werden, dasjenige herauszufinden, welches zur" κατ' ἐξοχήν die „Philoſophie der Gegenwart*“ zu heißen würdig wäre. Denn ſintemal wir Europäer unſtreitig die cultivirteſten Menſchen auf dem Erdboden ſind, in Europa aber Niemand eigentlich philoſophirt als nur in Deutſchland, in Deutſchland endlich nur Berlin (und allenfalls noch München) ohne Widerſpruch die berühmteſten Weltweiſen aufzuweiſen hat: ſo iſt ja (meint man) ſonnenklar, daß nur die Hegel'ſche (oder allenfalls die Schel- *) Es iſt überhaupt ſehr beruhigend zu vernehmen, aus dem Munde berühmter Weltweiſen zu vernehmen, daß was auch immer ein jeder Einzelne, was namentlich die Großen der Erde Thörichtes beginnen, welche Verſündigungen an der Menſchheit ſie ſich für ihre eigene Perſon zu Schuld mögen kommen laſſen: Alles doch nur für einen vernünftigen und nothwendigen Schritt vorwärts angeſehen werden müſſe !! — [p. 26] lingiſch » Hegel'ſche) Philoſophie allein die Philoſophie der Gegenwart genannt werden könne, während die übrigen Syſteme ſammt und ſonders als gar nicht daſeiend betrachtet werden müſſen. *)

It cannot be denied that there is something seductive in all these assertions. For, to dwell only on what is said about5 philosophy: how pleasant it is to think that we attain so cheaply, namely merely by acquainting ourselves with the system that happens to be dominant in the present, to the possession of the philosophy most perfect for this time, indeed (according to C.) even of the absolutely perfect philosophy! Nor, truly, do they make it hard for us to find out, among the several systems set up here and there in our days, which one would deserve to be called κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν [par excellence] the “philosophy of the present.” For since we Europeans are indisputably the most cultivated people on the face of the earth, and in Europe no one really philosophises except in Germany, and in Germany, finally, only Berlin (and at a pinch Munich too) can without contradiction point to the most celebrated philosophers: it is surely (so they think) as clear as day that only the Hegelian (or at a pinch the Schellingian-Hegelian) [p. 26] philosophy alone can be called the philosophy of the present, while the other systems one and all must be regarded as not existing at all.6

Doch wollen die Leſer nicht erſt unterſuchen, ob die Prämiſſen, auf welchen dieſe erfreulichen Behauptungen beruhen, auch alle richtig ſind?

But will the readers not first examine whether the premises on which these gratifying assertions rest are all correct as well?

Daß es ein allmäliges Fortſchreiten in der Vollkommenheit gebe, nehmen auch wir an; ja wir behaupten, daß ein ſolches ſtatt finde bei einer jeden endlichen Subſtanz im Einzelnen ſowohl als auch bei unſerem menſchlichen Geſchlechte im Ganzen, behaupten dieſes aus Gründen, deren ” wir uns ſehr deutlich bewußt ſind, obgleich wir ſie hierorts nur flüchtig andeuten können, Bei jeder endlichen Subſtanz (das Wort: Subſtanz nicht in dem ſchwankenden Sinne der modernen Philoſophie, ſondern im ſtrengen Sinne genommen, wo es ein Wirkliches bezeichnet, das keine Adhärenz an einem andern Wirklichen und eben deshalb einfach, unzerſtörbar u. f. w. iſt) bei jeder endlichen Subſtanz alſo beruhet die Möglichkeit eines in das Unendliche reichenden Fortſchreitens weſentlich auf dem Umſtande, daß eine jede ſolche Subſtanz veränderlich iſt und eine Vorſtellungskraft beſitzt, vermittels deren ſie durch die Einwirkung der ſie umgebenden andern ſtets neue Vorſtellungen erhält, daher mindeſtens in Einem Stücke, das die Bedingung zu allem Uebrigen enthält, in der Summe ihrer Vorſtellungen unaufhörlich zunimmt. **) Ein allmäliges Fortſchreiten bei der ganzen Menſchheit ſtellt ſich uns dar als möglich, ſofern die Erfahrungen, die eine frühere Zeit macht, von einer ſpätern, welcher ſie überliefert wurden, benützt werden können, um dadurch immer weiſer und klüger, *) Seit unſer Verf. dies niedergeſchrieben, hat ſich hierin allerdings Manches geändert. Der Herausg.

That there is a gradual progress in perfection we too assume; indeed we assert that such progress takes place in every finite substance individually as well as in our human race as a whole, and we assert this on grounds of which we are very clearly aware, although we can only hint at them fleetingly here. In every finite substance (the word substance taken not in the vacillating sense of modern philosophy but in the strict sense, in which it designates an actual thing that has no adherence in another actual thing and is for that very reason simple, indestructible, and so on), in every finite substance, then, the possibility of a progress reaching into the infinite rests essentially on the circumstance that every such substance is changeable and possesses a power of ideation, by means of which it constantly receives new ideas through the influence of the others surrounding it, and therefore, at least in one respect which contains the condition for all the rest, namely in the sum of its ideas, increases unceasingly. A gradual7 progress of mankind as a whole presents itself to us as possible in so far as the experiences that an earlier time makes can be used by a later one, to which they have been handed down, in order thereby to become ever wiser and more prudent, and hence also better and happier. But that this merely possible progress, after many temporary interruptions and regressions, will never fail altogether, at least not in mankind as a whole, as also in every individual substance once it has been brought so far as to will the good in earnest: this we expect partly on the ground that increasing knowledge joined with good will must gradually always result in an increase in every other perfection as well, partly because we believe that every good which is not in itself impossible will one day attain to actuality through God’s almighty governance of the whole. If these are the only grounds for which we expect a progress in every individual as well as in our race as a whole, then it is self-evident that, and why, this progress need not be an uninterrupted one. And this agrees too with experience, according to which it would be hard to demonstrate that, for example, every individual human being becomes uninterruptedly, and thus necessarily with every hour as well, somewhat more perfect; so that, for example, when he falls asleep in the evening tired from his day’s work, he would be and become more perfect during that very sleep than he was the day before in his waking state, and the next day again more perfect on waking than he had been in his sleep, etc. Stranger still is it to think that those among us who are so unfortunate as to sink into follies and vices would become more perfect even then, when all the world judges that they grow worse with every day. And are we perhaps to say even of the madman that he is now more perfect than he was before his misfortune? — Here, then, we should already have fallen into contradiction with the assertions of the modern school; for according to it every succeeding state of mankind would have to be regarded as a true progress, and thus presumably as a more perfect state than the one immediately preceding. It is true that they try to make this credible to us by the remark that every later state of the whole of mankind can be regarded as the result of all the preceding ones, and thus as the development arising out of them: and were this quite correct, then we ourselves too would not be disinclined to call the later state always a more perfect one. But they seem to have forgotten that the complete ground from which any later state of a thing emerges includes not merely the sum of all its earlier states but also the sum of all its relations and of all the forces acting upon it from without. In these relations, then, in these influences coming from without, it may lie that the development turns out one way in one case and otherwise in another, and does not every time deserve the name of a true progress towards perfection. According to the modern philosophers it would indeed be the Absolute, or God himself, who develops in the universe and attains to self-consciousness in mankind in particular; and then, to be sure, there would be no influences from without to be feared that might disturb the regularity of this course of development. But the assumption of such a God cannot be justified at all. Not only the originator of this whole newer manner of philosophising, Schelling, but even several of the most gifted of Hegel’s pupils, such as C. H. Fichte, Weisse and others, have long since renounced this pantheistic doctrine again, inasmuch as they acknowledge in the most unequivocal manner that there is a being to which real totality or absoluteness belongs; that this being is to be thought of not as one that first works its way up to self-consciousness in man, but as one endowed with self-consciousness from all eternity; that this thinking being is the ground of all other existence, or the creator of the world, and so on. — However, no attack on your pantheism is needed in order to show you contradictions, or at least arbitrary assumptions, in your doctrine of the development of the world spirit in mankind as a whole. Is the human race perhaps the only kind of beings in the universe that are rational, that you continually say that here and here alone the world spirit comes to its self-consciousness? But if (as no one can deny) the human race is surrounded by a countless multitude of other beings, rational and irrational; and if (as is equally settled) these other beings too have an influence on the shaping of our destinies:8 by what right do you presuppose that the history of mankind must follow so regular a course? By the very same right by which one would also have to be able to assert that the history of every single people, indeed of every family, of every society however arbitrarily assumed, of several persons as well as of every individual by himself, must present a regular whole. If you lack the courage to assert that the life of every single human being, a life which has often lasted only a few hours and has often been determined in its course by the most accidental external influences, represents a regular progress, a development of the world spirit: then neither may you assert with full confidence that the history of mankind as a whole presents such a development. And is it even a thought that will bear a bright light, this thought of yours that one and the same spirit, namely what you call the world spirit, appears in a plurality of individuals, namely in every single human being, and attains to its consciousness in them? One could speak so if the sensations, thoughts and actions of the individuals were at the same time sensations, thoughts and actions of this spirit appearing in them. Now I know, to be sure, that the sensations which my sore finger causes me may be accompanied, perhaps even (in part at least) produced, by certain other (possibly similar) sensations which my finger itself (the soul dwelling in it) has. And in a similar way you may, if you otherwise have grounds for it, imagine a spiritual being (the soul of the earth, say) which undergoes certain changes through everything that we human beings feel, think and do here; you may even assume that every sensation or idea which we human beings have produces a more or less similar sensation or idea in the world spirit as well. But what you can in no case assert without uttering the most manifest absurdity is that the sensations, ideas, etc. which we human beings have are themselves already sensations, ideas, etc. in this world spirit; or, what amounts to the same thing, that this world spirit attains to its self-consciousness not merely through us but — in us; that our thoughts are not certain similar thoughts merely occasioned in it, but are in themselves its thoughts.

**) Die nähere Auseinandersetzung dieſer wichtigen Lehren ſ. B/s Athanaſia. S. 85 = 91, Der Herausg, [p. 27] somit auch beſſer und glücklicher zu werden. Daß aber dieſes blos mögliche Fortſchreiten nach vielen zeitweiligen Unterbrechungen und Rückſchritten nie ganz ausbleiben werde, mindeſtens nicht bei der Menſchheit im Ganzen, wie auch bei jeder einzelnen Subſtanz, wenn ſie nur einmal ſo weit gebracht iſt, das Gute ernſtlich zu wollen, erwarten wir theils aus dem Grunde, weil zunehmende Erkenntniß bei gutem Willen allmälig immer auch ein Zunehmen in jeder andern Vollkommenheit zur Folge haben muß, theils weil wir glauben, daß jegliches Gute, ſo nicht an ſich unmöglich iſt, durch Gottes allmächtige Leitung des Ganzen einmal zur Wirklichkeit gelange. Noch wunderlicher iſt es zu denken, daß diejenigen aus uns, welche ſo unglücklich ſind, in Thorheiten und Laſter zu verſinken, auch da noch vollkommener würden, wo alle Welt urtheilt, daß ſie mit jedem Tage ſich verſchlimmern. Und ſollen wir etwa selbſt von dem Wahnſinnigen ſagen, daß er jetzt vollkommener ſei, als er vor ſeinem Unglück geweſen? =- Hier alſo wären wir ſchon in einen Widerſpruch mit den Behauptungen der modernen Schule gerathen; denn nach dieſer müßte ſich jeder folgende Zuſtand der Menſchheit als ein wahrer Fortſchritt, alſo wohl als ein vollkommenerer Zuſtand als der nächſtvorhergehende betrachten laſſen. Zwar ſucht man uns dies glaublich zu machen durch die [p. 28] Bemerkung, daß jeder ſpätere Zuſtand der ganzen Menſchheit als das Reſultat aller vorhergehenden, ſomit als die aus ihnen ſich ergebende Entwickelung betrachtet werden könne: und wäre dies ganz- richtig, dann wären auch wir ſelbſt nicht abgeneigt, den ſpätern Zuſtand ſtets einen vollkommenern zu nennen. Allein man ſcheint vergeſſen zu haben, daß zu dem vollſtändigen Grunde, aus welchem irgend ein ſpäterer Zuſtand eines Dinges hervortritt, nicht blos die Summe aller ſeiner früheren Zuſtände, ſondern auch die Summe aller ſeiner Verhältniſſe und aller auf dasſelbe von Außen einwirkenden Kräfte gehört. In dieſen Verhältniſſen nun, in dieſen von Außen kommenden Einwirkungen kann es liegen, daß die Entwickelung bei dem Einen ſo, bei dem Andern anders ausfällt, und nicht eben jedesmal den Namen eines wahren Fortſchrittes zur Vollkommenheit verdient. Nach den modernen Philoſophen wäre es zwar das Abſolute oder Gott ſelbſt, der ſich im Weltall entwickelt und in der Menſchheit insbeſondere zum Selbſtbewußtſein gelangt; und da wären denn freilich keine Einwirkungen von Außen, welche die Regelmäßigkeit dieſes Entwickelungsganges ſtören ſollten, zu fürchten, Aber die Annahme eines ſolchen Gottes läßt ſich überhaupt nicht rechtfertigen. Nicht nur der Urheber dieſer ganzen neueren Weiſe des Philoſophirens, Schelling, ſondern ſelbſt mehrere der geiſtreichſten Schüler Hegel’s, wie C. H. Fichte, Weiße u. A. haben ſich lange ſchon wieder losgeſagt von dieſer pantheiſtiſchen Lehre, indem ſie aufs Unzweideutigſte anerkennen, daß es ein Weſen gebe, welchem reale Totalität oder Abſolutheit zukommt, daß man dies Weſen nicht als ein zum Selbſtbewußtſein ſich erſt im Menſchen emporarbeitendes, ſondern als ein von jeher mit Selbſtbewußtſein begabtes zu denken habe, daß dieſes denkende Weſen der Grund alles andern Daſeins oder Weltſchöpfer ſei, u. s. w. = Indeſſen es bedarf nicht erſt eines Angriffs auf euern Pantheismus, um euch Wiederſprüche oder mindeſtens Willkürlichkeiten in euerer Lehre von der Entwickelung des Weltgeiſtes [p. 29] im Ganzen der Menſchheit nachzuweiſen. Iſt denn das Menſchengeſchlecht etwa die einzige Gattung von Weſen, welche vernünftig ſind im Weltall, daß ihr fortwährend ſprecht, hier und nur hier allein komme der Weltgeiſt zu ſeinem Selbſtbewußtſein? Wenn aber (wie Niemand zu leugnen vermag) das Geſchlecht der Menſchen von einer zahlloſen Menge anderer Weſen, vernünftiger und vernunftloſer, umgeben iſt; und wenn (wie gleichfalls ausgemacht iſt) auch dieſe anderen Weſen Einfluß auf die Geſtaltung unſerer Schickſale haben: *) mit welchem Rechte ſetzt ihr voraus, daß die Geſchichte der Menſchheit einen ſo regelmäßigen Verlauf beobachten müſſe? Mit ebendemſelben Rechte, mit dem ſich auch müßte behaupten laſſen, daß die Geſchichte jedes einzelnen Volkes, ja jeder Familie, jeder noch ſo beliebig angenommenen Geſellſchaft, Mehrerer ſowohl als auch eines jeden Individuums für ſich allein ein regelmäßiges Ganze darbieten müſſe. Habt ihr den Muth nicht zu behaupten, daß eines jeden ein- -. zelnen Menſchen Leben, ein Leben, welches oft nur wenige Stunden gedauert, oft durchdie zufälligſten äußern Einwirkungen in ſeinem Verlaufe beſtimmt worden iſt, ein regelmäßiges Fortſchreiten, eine Entwickelung des Weltgeiſtes. darſtelle: ſo dürft ihr auch nicht mit voller Zuverſicht behaupten, daß die Geſchichte der Menſchheit im Ganzen eine ſolche Entwickelung darbiete. Und iſt es denn auch nur ein Gedanke, der eine helle Beleuchtung verträgt, euer Gedanke, daß ein und derſelbe Geiſt, nämlich der von euch ſo genannte Weltgeiſt in einer Mehrzahl von Individuen, nämlich in jedem einzelnen Menſchen erſcheine, zu ſeinem Bewußtſein in ihnen gelange? So ließe ſich sprechen, wenn die Empfindungen, Gedanken und Handlungen der Individuen zugleich Empfindungen, Gedanken und Handlungen dieſes in ihnen erſcheinenden Geiſtes ſelbſt wären. Nun weiß ich allerdings, daß die Empfindungen, die mir *) Einer geringen Veränderung in der Miſchung der Elemente können ganze Völkerſchaften erliegen, [p. 30] mein wunder Finger verurſacht, begleitet, wohl gar (theilweiſe wenigſtens) hervorgebracht ſein können. von gewiſſen andern (allenfalls ähnlichen) Empfindungen, welche mein Finger ſelbſt (die in ihm wohnende Seele deſſelben) hat. Und auf ähnliche Weiſe möget ihr "denn, wenn ihr ſonſt Gründe dazu habt, ein geiſtiges Weſen euch denken (etwa die Seele der Erde), welches gewiſſe Veränderungen durch Alles das erleide, was wir Menſchen hier empfinden, denken und thun; ihr möget ſogar annehmen, daß jede Empfindung oder Vorſtellung, welche wir Menſchen haben, eine mehr oder weniger ähnliche Empfindung oder Vorſtellung auch in dem Weltgeiſte erzeuge. Was ihr aber auf keinen Fall behaupten könnet, ohne die offenbarſte Ungereimtheit zu ſagen, iſt, daß die Empfindungen, Vorſtellungen ꝛc., welche wir Menſchen haben, ſelbſt ſchon Empfindungen, Vorſtellungen ꝛc. in dieſem Weltgeiſte wären; oder was eben ſo viel iſt, daß dieſer Weltgeiſt nicht etwa blos durch uns, ſondern — in uns zu ſeinem Selbſtbewußtſein gelange, daß unſere Gedanken nicht gewiſſe ähnliche, nur in ihm veranlaßte, ſondern an ſich ſelbſt ſeine Gedanken wären.

1 [p. 30] [p. 29] [p. 28] [p. 27] The matter would, of course, stand somewhat differently if Bolzano had been able to carry out what he later hinted at in passing, with regard to the second treatise, on a supplementary sheet of his manuscript (printed at the end of this treatise); but then not merely an improved, but an entirely new treatise would have come out of his essay. 2 In Salzmann’s Schwarzmantel. 3 As, for example, Dr. Mayer recently said of the Hegelian philosophy in a work written in a very popular vein, in a Schreiben an eine Dame über Philosophie (Berlin 1837). 4 Prolegomena zur Historiosophie. Berlin 1838. 5 It is altogether most reassuring to hear, to hear from the mouths of celebrated philosophers, that whatever folly each individual, and especially the great ones of the earth, may undertake, whatever sins against mankind they may allow themselves to commit in their own persons: all of it must nevertheless be regarded only as a rational and necessary step forward!! — 6 Since our author wrote this, much has indeed changed in this respect.
